

## Cohort 1 Footnotes

[^1]: The *Typikon of the Great Church of Christ* (*Τυπικὸν τῆς τοῦ Χριστοῦ Μεγάλης Ἐκκλησίας*), compiled by George Violakis (Protopsaltis of the Ecumenical Patriarchate) and published in Constantinople in 1888 under Patriarch Dionysios V, represents the definitive codification of the Neo-Sabaitic cathedral and parochial rite for the Greek-speaking Orthodox world. It superseded the earlier 1838 and 1851 editions compiled by Constantine the Protopsaltis.

[^2]: The frontispiece reproduces the famous late ninth-century lunette mosaic over the Imperial Door leading from the inner narthex into the nave of Hagia Sophia. Christ Pantokrator is enthroned in glory, with His right hand raised in blessing and His left holding an open Gospel codex with the Johannine incipits: *«Εἰρήνη ὑμῖν. Ἐγώ εἰμι τὸ φῶς τοῦ κόσμου»* (John 20:19, 26; John 8:12). Emperor Leo VI ("the Wise", r. 886–912) kneels in humble proskynesis before His feet, imploring divine mercy.

[^3]: Constantine Byzantios (d. 1862), Archon Protopsaltis of the Great Church of Christ, published the first official reformed patriarchal Typikon in Constantinople in 1838, re-edited in 1851. Violakis’s 1888 edition thoroughly systematized and revised Constantine’s texts with synodal authority.

[^4]: Protopresbyter Robert T. Athas (in Greek, Epameinondas Th. Athasakos) completed his translation of the Lenten Triodion rubrics of Violakis at St. Vladimir’s Orthodox Theological Seminary (Crestwood, NY) prior to this comprehensive collaborative edition.

[^5]: Presvytera (Greek: *Πρεσβυτέρα*) is the traditional title of respect accorded to the wife of an Eastern Christian presbyter (priest), corresponding to Church Slavonic *Panimatka* or *Matushka*.

[^6]: 1 Corinthians 14:40: *«πάντα δὲ εὐσχημόνως καὶ κατὰ τάξιν γινέσθω»*. In the liturgical tradition of the Christian East, this apostolic precept forms the foundational theological rationale for the *taxis* (Slavonic: *chyn*, *ustav*) governing divine worship.

[^7]: Theodore Bogdanos (Professor Emeritus, University of Georgia) was a distinguished musicologist, scholar, and protopsaltis whose manual *The Byzantine Liturgy: Hymnology and Order* (Denver, 1993) provided the musical and rubrical framework for liturgical synthesis between the Julian and Gregorian calendar reckonings.


## Cohort 2 Footnotes

[^8]: Protopresbyter Robert T. Athas (Epameinondas Th. Athasakos), principal translator of the Violakis Typikon, studied under Alexander Schmemann and John Meyendorff at St. Vladimir’s Orthodox Theological Seminary, where his pioneering Master of Divinity thesis (*The Triodion in the Typicon of the Great Church*, 1974) provided the initial impetus for this monumental thirty-year bilingual publication project.

[^9]: Ekphonetic cantillation (*ἐκφωνητική*) represents the ancient Byzantine solemn liturgical recitation of the Holy Scriptures (the Apostolos and the Gospel), characterized by specialized neumatic inflection marks (*ekphonetic neumes*) guiding pitch accent, cadence, and solemnity. Protopresbyter Meletios Diacandrew (consecrated Bishop of Aristea in 1993) preserved this rigorous patriarchal oral tradition.

[^10]: Metropolitan Isaiah (Chronopoulos) of Denver was enthroned as Bishop of Denver in 1992 and elevated to Metropolitan in 2002. His official hierarchic endorsement underscores the pastoral necessity of an authoritative, bilingual English-Greek recension of the Violakis Typikon to safeguard the uniformity of Byzantine liturgical praxis across the parishes of the Greek Orthodox Archdiocese of America.

[^11]: The traditional liturgical prayer for the reposed (*Aionia i mnimi* / *Вѣчная память* / *Memory Eternal*) derives from Psalm 111:6 (LXX) and the Orthodox Funeral and Memorial Services (*Mnemosynon* / *Panakhyda*). It is inscribed here as an episcopal blessing upon the late Dr. George T. Demos (1934–2012), editor and compiler of this bilingual edition.

[^12]: The Lenten Triodion (*Τριῴδιον*) contains the liturgical propers from the Sunday of the Publican and Pharisee through Holy Saturday. Fr. Athas's thesis critically examined how George Violakis codified the ancient cathedral rite of Constantinople for parish use, synthesizing the monastic Typikon of Saint Sabbas (*Hagio-Sabaitic* synthesis) with the urban pastoral needs of nineteenth-century parishes.

[^13]: *«Τέλος καὶ τῷ Θεῷ δόξα!»* ("The end and glory to God!") is the time-honored Greek scribal doxological formula (*teleutaios stichos*), universally appended by Byzantine copyists, liturgists, and typikographers at the explicit of a completed codex, affirming that divine wisdom is both the source and terminus of all sacred labor.

[^14]: The Greek half-title *ΜΕΤΑΦΡΑΣΙΣ ΤΥΠΙΚΟΥ* (*Translation of the Typikon*) formally introduces the core liturgical translation section of the work, separating the preliminary front matter and introductory commentaries from the operative rubrical text.

[^15]: This phonetic table reflects the modern standard Greek (Demotic/Katharevousa) pronunciation system employed across the Ecumenical Patriarchate and the Greek Orthodox Archdiocese of America, differing fundamentally from the reconstructed classical Erasmian phonology by observing iotacism (whereby *η, ι, υ, ει, οι, υι* converge upon the vowel sound [i]) and soft voiced spirants.

[^16]: The editorial convention establishes a rigorous, three-tier typographic taxonomy: standard parentheses `(...)` preserve the original 1888 text of George Violakis; curly brackets `{...}` designate translator additions inserted by Fr. Robert Athas to elucidate elliptic rubrics; and square brackets `[...]` contain critical expansions, biblical citations, and cross-references contributed by editors Dr. George Demos and Dr. Theodore Bogdanos.

[^17]: The Patriarchal Synodal Commission of 1888 was convoked under Ecumenical Patriarch Dionysios V to undertake a comprehensive critical review of the existing liturgical typika in active use—principally the 1838 and 1851 editions compiled by Archon Protopsaltis Constantine Byzantios. Composed of four senior metropolitans (Methodios of Ainos, Gabriel of Samos, Procopios of Caesarea, and Methodios of Mytiline) alongside Archon Protopsaltis George Violakis, the Commission resolved hundreds of contradictions and ambiguities between printed books and lived patriarchal cathedral practice.

[^18]: The Ninth Hour (*Enati Ora* / *Девятый час*), commemorating the Death of Christ upon the Cross at 3:00 PM, constitutes the canonical capstone of the daily daytime cursus. Because the liturgical day ends at sunset, the Ninth Hour belongs to the preceding day. The Commission codified its mandatory celebration prior to Great Vespers on the Sunday of St. Thomas (Antipascha) and before the Wednesday and Thursday Vespers of the Leavetaking of Pascha and the Ascension, establishing rubrical symmetry across the Paschal season.

[^19]: The Festal Dismissal (*Apolysis*) on Great Feasts of the Lord (*Despotikai Heortai*) takes the identical proper invocation at both Great Vespers and the Divine Liturgy. Archon Protopsaltis Constantine had argued in his 1838/1851 editions that the Resurrection formula *«Ὁ ἀναστὰς ἐκ νεκρῶν»* was strictly forbidden at Sunday of All Saints Vespers because Christ's Resurrection occurred at midnight. The 1888 Commission formally overturned Constantine's restriction, decreeing that Sunday Vespers participates in the resurrectional glory of the Lord's Day.


## Cohort 3 Footnotes

[^20]: Archon Protopsaltis Constantine Byzantios, in his 1838 and 1851 editions of the Typikon, had curtailed the commemoration of celebrated saints occurring on Sunday by prescribing solely the Sunday Resurrection Kathismata across the first two Psalter stichologies of Matins (*Orthros*), postponing any Kathisma of the saint to the conclusion of the second stichology or omitting it altogether (as for Prophet Elijah or Saint Marina). The 1888 Patriarchal Commission of George Violakis reformed this curtailment by intercalating one Kathisma of the celebrating saint into each of the first two stichologies and chanting the post-Polyeleos Kathismata after the third stichology, thereby restoring the ancient cathedral and monastic balance between the Sunday Resurrectional office and the Menaion feast.

[^21]: The 1838 Typikon of Constantine frequently employed the abbreviated rubric "the Beatitudes are said" (*λέγονται οἱ Μακαρισμοί*) or "and so forth" (*καὶ καθεξῆς*), without explicitly directing whether the troparia of Ode 6 of the Saint's canon were to be appended to the Resurrection Beatitudes (as on the 2nd and 5th Sundays of Great Lent) or omitted. This liturgical ellipsis caused recurring perplexity among parish clergy and cantors; the 1888 Violakis Typikon resolves this ambiguity by providing detailed rubrical specifications for every liturgical case.

[^22]: The Sunday Midnight Office (*Mesonyktikon* / *Полунощница*) celebrates Christ's nocturnal Resurrection from the dead. While the Great Horologion (*Mega Horologion*) directs that the Ypakoe (*Ὑπακοή*) of the Tone of the week be chanted on Sundays in place of the weekday penitential troparia, Constantine Byzantios had prescribed the three penitential troparia (*"Have mercy on us, Lord, have mercy on us"*) on all Sundays throughout the liturgical year. The 1888 Violakis Commission restored the Ypakoe for all ordinary Sundays and Sundays of the Pentecostarion, restricting the three penitential troparia strictly to the penitential cycle of the Lenten Triodion.

[^23]: *Chasmodia* (*χασμῳδία*), denoting in classical Greek rhetoric and liturgical musicology a hiatus, audible disharmony, or choir disorder caused by singers scrambling through disparate liturgical volumes (Menaia, Festal Horologion, Anthologia), was a persistent problem in nineteenth-century parish choirs. By printing the complete texts of the Festal Antiphons and their corresponding Megalynaria directly in the body of the Typikon, the Commission ensured smooth and uninterrupted celebration of the Divine Liturgy.

[^24]: Question 1 submitted by the 1888 Patriarchal Commission to Ecumenical Patriarch Dionysios V and the Holy Synod concerned the calendar concurrence when December 24 (Paramoni of the Nativity of Christ) falls on a Sunday. Under this concurrence, the Royal Hours are transferred to Friday and the Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom is celebrated on Sunday morning. Patriarch Dionysios V and the Holy Synod formally ratified the rule that the Sunday Resurrectional hymns are omitted at Saturday Great Vespers to preserve the solemnity of the Paramoni. The 1685 Venetian edition cited by the Commission is the *Typikon of the Ecclesiastical Service* (*Τυπικὸν τῆς ἐκκλησιαστικῆς ἀκολουθίας*).

[^25]: Question 2 resolved the selection of the Troparion for the Royal Hours of the Nativity and Theophany when either feast occurs on a Sunday or Monday. Because the Royal Hours cannot be celebrated on a Saturday or Sunday, they are anticipated on Friday morning. The Holy Synod decreed that on that Friday the Forefeast Troparia are chanted (*"Make ready, Bethlehem"* / *"Make ready, Zabulon"*) rather than the proper Troparia of the Eve (*"When recorded in the census"* / *"The Jordan River turned back"*).

[^26]: Question 3 addressed the Paschal Entrance Hymn (*Eisodikon* / *Входный стих*) *«Ἐν ἐκκλησίαις εὐλογεῖτε τὸν Θεόν, Κύριον ἐκ πηγῶν Ἰσραήλ»* (Psalm 67:27 LXX: *"In assemblies bless God, the Lord from the fountains of Israel"*). Although rubrics in certain printed editions of the Pentikostarion suggested reverting to the ordinary Little Entrance chant *«Δεῦτε προσκυνήσωμεν»* (*"Come, let us worship"*) on weekdays following Thomas Sunday, Patriarch Dionysios V and the Synod established that the proper Paschal Eisodikon must be chanted at every Divine Liturgy celebrated through the Apodosis of Pascha (*Ἀπόδοσις*).

[^27]: Question 4 addressed the abusive practice in nineteenth-century Constantinopolitan parishes of omitting the singing of the **"Alleluia"** (*Ἀλληλούϊα*) and its accompanying psalm verses after the reading of the Acts of the Apostles during Bright Week (*Diakainesimos*). Patriarch Dionysios V and the Holy Synod reaffirmed the ancient order of the Pentikostarion, ruling that the chanting of the Alleluia with its appointed verses is mandatory and must not be omitted.

[^28]: Question 5 resolved the initial sacerdotal exclamation opening Vespers and Matins throughout Bright Week. Older typika and the 1685 Venice edition appointed the ordinary blessing *«Εὐλογητὸς ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν»* (*"Blessed is our God"*), whereas the 1838 Typikon of Constantine had introduced the solemn initial doxology *«Δόξα τῇ ἁγίᾳ καὶ ὁμοουσίῳ καὶ ζωοποιῷ καὶ ἀδιαιρέτῳ Τριάδι»* (*"Glory to the holy, and consubstantial, and life-creating, and undivided Trinity"*). The Holy Synod ratified the use of *«Δόξα τῇ ἁγίᾳ...»* as the initial blessing for all daily offices during Bright Week, reflecting the continuous solemnity of the Paschal Vigil.

[^29]: Question 6 addressed the appointment of Pentecost hymnody during holy ordinations (*cheirotoniai* / *рукоположения*). The Patriarchal Archieratikon directs that during the candidate's triple circumambulation of the Holy Table (*Agia Trapeza*), the choir chants appointed troparia from Pentecost alongside the traditional ordination hymns (*"Holy Martyrs"*, *"Glory to Thee, Christ God"*, *"Rejoice, O Isaiah"*). The Holy Synod entrusted the definitive rubrical codification of these hymns to the Commission itself.

[^30]: Question 7 concerned the liturgical hymn chanted immediately following the canonical election of an Ecumenical Patriarch. The Commission recommended substituting the traditional Marian Megalynarion *«Ἄξιόν ἐστιν ὡς ἀληθῶς μακαρίζειν σὲ τὴν Θεοτόκον»* (*"It is truly right to bless thee, O Theotokos"*) with the Trinitarian hymn *«Ἄξιόν ἐστιν ὡς ἀληθῶς τὴν Ὑπέρθεον ὑμνεῖν Τριάδα»* (*"It is truly right to hymn the Supremely Divine Trinity"*). Patriarch Dionysios V and the Holy Synod rejected this proposed change, directing that the time-honored Marian hymn be preserved in accordance with established patriarchal tradition (*«διότι οὕτω συνειθίσθη»*).

[^31]: Canon LXXV (75) of the Council in Trullo (the Quinisext Ecumenical Council, convoked in Constantinople in 691–692 AD under Emperor Justinian II) constitutes the fundamental canonical legislation of the Eastern Orthodox Church governing church singing. The canon strictly prohibits unruly vocal shouting, artificial vocal distortion, and the introduction of secular, theatrical songs (*thymelika mele*) into divine worship, commanding that all sacred psalmody be offered with compunction (*katanyxis*) and inner spiritual vigilance to God, Who searches the hearts of men.

[^32]: John Zonaras (*Ἰωάννης Ζωναρᾶς*), renowned 12th-century Byzantine canonist, theologian, and chronicler, served as Great Drungarios of the Watch and First Imperial Secretary (*Protasekretis*) in Constantinople before retiring to the Monastery of Saint Glykeria. His authoritative commentary on Trullo Canon 75 interprets the scriptural mandate of piety given to the sons of Israel as applying preeminently to Christian chanters, vigorously condemning secularized theatrical melodies, theatrical posturing, and musical self-aggrandizement in the house of God.

[^33]: Theodore Balsamon (*Θεόδωρος Βαλσαμών*), titular Greek Orthodox Patriarch of Antioch and prominent 12th-century canonist, served as Nomophylax and Chartophylax of the Great Church of Hagia Sophia. In his commentary on Canon 75 of Trullo, Balsamon emphasizes that church buildings are consecrated houses of prayer, citing Canon 98 (often numbered 103 or 108 in Western codices) of the Council of Carthage (419 AD) and Saint John Chrysostom's homilies on Isaiah (*In illud: Vidi Dominum*, PG 56:97–142) to forbid unseemly physical gestures, dancing, and theatrical histrionics by cantors during sacred psalmody.


## Cohort 4 Footnotes

[^34]: Alexios Aristenos (*Ἀλέξιος Ἀριστηνός*), 12th-century Byzantine canonist who served as Nomophylax, Orphanotrophos, and Great Oikonomos of the Great Church of Hagia Sophia under Emperors John II Komnenos and Manuel I Komnenos, authored a concise, systematic commentary (*Epitomē*) on the Canons of the Apostles, the Ecumenical and Local Synods, and the Church Fathers. In his canonical gloss on Canon 75 of the Council in Trullo (691–692 AD), Aristenos establishes that vocal chanting in the sanctuary must be offered with deep spiritual compunction (*katanyxis*) and inner sobriety to God, Who examines the hidden thoughts of the heart, strictly proscribing cacophonous shouting, jagged cries, and secular vocal mannerisms that disrupt liturgical prayer.

[^35]: Saint John Chrysostom (*Ἰωάννης ὁ Χρυσόστομος*, c. 347–407 AD), Archbishop of Constantinople and Doctor of the Church. The passage excerpted here derives from his *Homily IV on Isaiah 6:1* (*In illud: Vidi Dominum homilia IV*, PG 56:120–126; Migne ed., Vol. 6, pp. 99–102). In this severe exhortation on liturgical dignity, Chrysostom contrasts the sublime, trembling worship of the Seraphim chanting the Trisagion before the Throne of God with the irreverent behaviour of worldly churchgoers who emulate actors, dancers, and mimes, hopping about and shouting in confusion. This patristic excerpt was omitted in the 1990s Denver bilingual printing and is critically restored here in accordance with the MTS-1 epigraphic mandate.

[^36]: Saint John Chrysostom, *Homily XI on the Gospel of Saint Matthew* (PG 57:193–202; Migne ed., Vol. 57, p. 200). Chrysostom cites the prophetic oracle of Isaiah (Isaiah 29:13 LXX: *«Ἐγγίζει μοι ὁ λαὸς οὗτος ἐν τῷ στόματι αὐτοῦ, καὶ ἐν τοῖς χείλεσιν αὐτῶν τιμῶσί με, ἡ δὲ καρδία αὐτῶν πόρρω ἀπέχει ἀπ' ἐμοῦ»*; quoted also by Christ in Matthew 15:8 and Mark 7:6) to admonish Christians who imagine that reciting two or three psalms casually and rushing through customary prayers mechanically without compunction or interior attention suffices for their salvation.

[^37]: Saint John Chrysostom, *Homily X on the Acts of the Apostles* (PG 60:85–92; Migne ed., Vol. 60). Chrysostom laments the spiritual frivolousness of congregations that attend divine services as though attending a theatrical spectacle of kitharodes and tragedians, where temporal entertainment produces no enduring spiritual fruit. He sharply contrasts the youth who eagerly memorize and sing demonic theater songs at home with the negligence of those who depart from the house of God without retaining a single divine hymn or prophetic word in their hearts.

[^38]: Saint John Chrysostom, *Homily XIX on the Epistle to the Ephesians* (PG 62:129–136; Migne ed., Vol. 62). Exegeting Ephesians 5:18–21 (*«Πληροῦσθε ἐν Πνεύματι, λαλοῦντες ἑαυτοῖς ψαλμοῖς καὶ ὕμνοις καὶ ᾠδαῖς πνευματικαῖς, ᾄδοντες καὶ ψάλλοντες ἐν τῇ καρδίᾳ ὑμῶν τῷ Κυρίῳ»*), Chrysostom defines liturgical psalmody as a spiritual cup of sobriety that dispels sinful drunkenness and sharpens mental clarity. He insists that true liturgical chanting requires active intellectual vigilance (*synesis*), warning that those who utter sacred words while allowing their hearts to wander in worldly distractions chant only with the lips and not unto the Lord.

[^39]: Saint Theodore the Studite (*Θεόδωρος ὁ Στουδίτης*, 759–826 AD), Abbot of the famous monastery of St. John the Baptist of Studion in Constantinople and champion of holy icons. The instruction excerpted here is preserved in his *Small Catecheses* (Catechesis 29/30: *To the Canonarch* / *«Πρὸς τὸν κανονάρχην»*). The canonarch (*κανονάρχης* / *канонарх*) directed the antiphonary psalmody, intoned the incipits of the Kathismata and Stichera, regulated the pitch and tempo of the two choirs (*choroi*), maintained melodic discipline without theatrical leaps or exaggerated dynamics, and instructed younger monks in ecclesiastical chant.

[^40]: Canon XV (15) of the Local Council of Laodicea (c. 363–364 AD) decreed: *«Περὶ τοῦ μὴ δεῖν πλὴν τῶν κανονικῶν ψαλτῶν, τῶν ἐπὶ τὸν ἄμβωνα ἀναβαινόντων καὶ ἀπὸ διφθέρας ψαλλόντων, ἑτέρους τινὰς ψάλλειν ἐν ἐκκλησίᾳ»* (*"No others shall sing in the church, save only the canonical singers, who go up upon the ambo and sing from a parchment book"*). This canonical boundary was enacted to preserve musical order (*eutaxia*) and textual solemnity in cathedral and parish assemblies, preventing uneducated or chaotic congregational shouting (*chasmodia*).

[^41]: The Patriarchal Music Committee (*Πατριαρχικὴ Μουσικὴ Ἐπιτροπή*) was established in Constantinople in 1881–1883 under Ecumenical Patriarch Joachim III (1878–1884, 1901–1912) to reform church music notation, standardize liturgical melodies, and establish choral discipline. Its members included leading musicologists and cantors of the Patriarchate. The seven governing criteria for liturgical choirs cited by George Violakis were published in the official periodical of the Ecumenical Patriarchate, *Ekklisiastiki Alitheia* (*Ἐκκλησιαστικὴ Ἀλήθεια*), 8th Year (1883), pp. 119–121, mandating acoustic vocal blend, mandatory rehearsals of *isokrates* and canonarchs, sacred deportment, intelligible enunciation, explicit rhythmic divisions (*tomai*), and notation of the drone (*ison*).


## Cohort 5 Footnotes

[^42]: The dual-column pagination schema of the 1990s Denver bilingual edition coordinates two distinct text traditions. The first column reproduces the printed pagination of the official 1888 Constantinople editio princeps of George Violakis (*Τυπικὸν τῆς τοῦ Χριστοῦ Μεγάλης Ἐκκλησίας*, Constantinople: Patriarchal Press, 1888), which spans pages 1 through 445. The second column designates the facing bilingual pagination of the Denver publication (Greek original text on even-numbered folios, English translation on facing odd-numbered folios, pages 1 through 937), concluding with the specialized Dictionary of Typikon Terms (*Λεξικὸν τοῦ Τυπικοῦ*, pp. TD-1–TD-88). The front-matter leaves use Greek alphabetic numerals (`α΄` to `ιγ΄`) in the first column aligned with Roman lower-case and Arabic numerals (`i` to `27`) in the second column.

[^43]: The *Protheoria* (*Προθεωρία τοῦ Τυπικοῦ*, "Pretheory of the Typikon" or General Rubrics) constitutes the liturgical foundation of both the Byzantine and Ruthenian typikon traditions. Rooted in the ancient Sabbaite and Studite monastic codes, the *Protheoria* systematizes the invariable statutory sequence, bodily actions, and ceremonial movements of the daily cycle of services—the Ninth Hour (*Θ΄ Ὥρα*), Daily and Great Vespers (*Ἑσπερινός*), the Midnight Office (*Μεσονυκτικόν*), Matins (*Ὄρθρος*), and the Divine Liturgy (*Θεία Λειτουργία*)—establishing canonical rules for incipits, kathismata, psalmody, stichera, the Lucernarium, antiphons, and dismissals prior to the specific variable propers of the liturgical year.

[^44]: The Introductory Psalm (*Προοιμιακὸς Ψαλμός*, Psalm 103 LXX: **"Bless the Lord, O my soul"** / *Εὐλόγει ἡ ψυχή μου τὸν Κύριον* / *Благослови, душе моя, Господа*). In Byzantine and UGCC usage, this psalm opens Great and Daily Vespers, praising the wisdom and majesty of God in the creation of the cosmos. During its recitation or chanting, the priest, uncovered and standing before the closed holy doors, reads the seven silent Prayers of the Lucernarium (*Εὐχαὶ τοῦ Λυχνικοῦ*) from the *Sluzhebnik* (*Ἱερατικόν*), recalling the exile of Adam outside paradise praying for redemption.

[^45]: The First Kathisma of the Psalter (*Α΄ Κάθισμα τοῦ Ψαλτηρίου*, Psalms 1–8 LXX: **"Blessed is the man"** / *Μακάριος ἀνήρ* / *Блаженъ мужъ*) and its First Stasis (*Α΄ Στάσις*, Psalms 1–3 LXX). In ancient monastic typika, the entire Psalter is divided into twenty kathismata, with the First Kathisma appointed for Saturday Great Vespers. In the Constantinopolitan parish reform codified by Constantine the Protopsaltis and George Violakis, the chanting of the First Kathisma is reserved for Great Feasts and Saturday evenings, with ordinary parish practice frequently reciting or chanting only the First Stasis (*«Μακάριος ἀνήρ»*), an urban condensation documented throughout the *Protheoria*.

[^46]: Psalm 140 LXX (**"Lord, I have cried unto Thee, hear me"** / *«Κύριε ἐκέκραξα πρὸς σέ, εἰσάκουσόν μου»* / *«Господи, воззвахъ къ Тебѣ, услыши мя»*), followed by Psalms 141, 129, and 116 LXX, forming the ancient Lucernarium (Lamp-lighting) psalmody of Vespers. Violakis specifies the precise melodic intonation according to the Tone of the week (Tone 1 to Tone 8) and regulates the distribution of the 10, 8, or 6 appointed Stichera between the Octoechos, the Menaion, and the Triodion, accompanied by the evening censing of the entire temple and iconostasis.

[^47]: The Artoklasia (*Ἀρτοκλασία* / *Лития и благословеніе хлѣбовъ*, Blessing of the Loaves) is celebrated at the conclusion of the Litiya at Great Vespers on the eve of Great Feasts. In the Ruthenian and UGCC tradition, a table or the Tetrapod in the center of the nave holds a vessel containing five loaves of bread, wheat, wine, and oil. The priest, accompanied by the deacon censing the offerings, chants the ancient troparion **"Rejoice, O Virgin Mother of God"** (*Θεοτόκε Παρθένε*) and intones the prayer of blessing: **"Lord Jesus Christ our God, Who didst bless the five loaves in the wilderness and therewith didst satisfy the five thousand..."**

[^48]: The Resurrectional Evlogitaria (*Εὐλογητάρια*, Psalm 118:12 LXX: **"Blessed art Thou, O Lord, teach me Thy statutes"** / *Εὐλογητὸς εἶ, Κύριε, δίδαξόν με τὰ δικαιώματά σου* / *Благословенъ еси, Господи, научи мя оправданіемъ Твоимъ*), chanted in Tone 5 on Sundays, followed by the Ypakoe (*Ὑπακοή*), the Anavathmoi (*Ἀναβαθμοί*, the Hymns of Ascents based on the Gradual Psalms 119–133 LXX), the Prokimenon, and the reading of one of the eleven Resurrection Gospels of Matins (*Ἑωθινὸν Εὐαγγέλιον*), commemorating the manifestations of the risen Christ.

[^49]: The Matins Canon (*Κανών*), structured upon the nine biblical canticles (Odes / *ᾠδαί*). Violakis details the variable insertion of the Katavasiai (*Καταβασίαι*), the Small Ektenia and Kathisma after Ode 3, the Kontakion and Ikos (*Κοντάκιον καὶ Οἶκος*) after Ode 6, the Magnificat (*Μεγαλύνει ἡ ψυχή μου τὸν Κύριον*, Luke 1:46–55) with the refrain **"More honorable than the Cherubim"** (*Τὴν τιμιωτέραν τῶν Χερουβίμ*) preceding Ode 9, followed by the Exapostilaria (*Ἐξαποστειλάρια*), the Praises (Lauds / *Αἶνοι*, Psalms 148–150 LXX), and the solemn Great Doxology (*Δοξολογία Μεγάλη*).

[^50]: The Divine Liturgy (*Ἡ Θεία Λειτουργία*). Violakis outlines the statutory entrance into the Eucharistic liturgy, distinguishing the recitation of the Typika (Psalms 102 and 145 LXX) and the Beatitudes (*Μακαρισμοί*, Matthew 5:3–12) on ordinary Sundays from the Daily Antiphons and the Festal Antiphons of the Great Feasts. The rubrics govern the Little Entrance with the Holy Gospel Book, the singing of the Eisodikon (**"Come, let us worship and bow down before Christ..."** / *Δεῦτε προσκυνήσωμεν*), the appointed post-entrance Troparia and Kontakia, the Trisagion (*Ἅγιος ὁ Θεός*), the Epistle and Gospel readings, the Cherubic Hymn (*Χερουβικὸς Ὕμνος*), the hymn to the Mother of God **"It is truly meet"** (*Ἄξιόν ἐστιν*), the Communion Hymn (*Κοινωνικόν*), and the concluding dismissals.

[^51]: The annual cycles of the Byzantine liturgical year: The Menaion (*Μηναῖον*), comprising twelve volumes governing the immovable calendar from September 1 (the Indiction or Ecclesiastical New Year) through August 31; and the Triodion (*Τριῴδιον*), governing the ten-week movable penitential season preceding Holy Pascha. The Triodion spans from the pre-Lenten Sundays (Publican and Pharisee, Prodigal Son, Meatfare, and Cheesefare) through the forty days of Great Lent and Great and Holy Week, regulating the Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete, the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts, and Great Compline.

[^52]: The Pentecostarion (*Πεντηκοστάριον* / Paschal Florilegium / *Цвѣтная Тріодь*), the liturgical book regulating the fifty-day festive cycle extending from the Resurrection of Christ on Holy Pascha through the Sunday of All Saints (the First Sunday after Pentecost). This joyous season encompasses Bright Week (*Διακαινήσιμος Ἑβδομάς*), Antipascha (St. Thomas Sunday), the Sunday of the Myrrh-bearers, Mid-Pentecost (*Μεσοπεντηκοστή*), the Ascension of the Lord, and the Holy Fathers of the First Ecumenical Council in Nicaea (A.D. 325).

[^53]: The Appendix (*Παράρτημα τοῦ Τυπικοῦ*). This specialized section codifies patriarchal ceremonial rites and episcopal services unique to the Great Church of Constantinople: the Patriarchal celebrations of the Akathist Hymn and the Twelve Passion Gospels, Church Consecrations, the Enthronement and Return of the Ecumenical Patriarch, the Indiction service on September 1, liturgical schedules (*Horologion*), episcopal ordinations (*Cheirotonia*), conciliar concelebrations (*Sylleitourgon*), funeral and memorial services (*Mnemosyna*), and the *Eortodromion* (*Ἑορτοδρόμιον* / Table of Feasts), which mathematically harmonizes the eighteen immovable feasts occurring between January 11 and May 25 with the movable dates of the Triodion and Pentecostarion.


## Cohort 6 Footnotes

[^54]: The Ninth Hour (*Θ΄ Ὥρα*) in the Byzantine and Ruthenian synthesis. Commemorating the death of Christ upon the Cross at the ninth hour of the day (approx. 3:00 PM; Matthew 27:46–50), the Ninth Hour occupies a canonical pivot in the daily liturgical cycle. As the concluding office of the preceding liturgical day, it seals the diurnal services before Vespers introduces the liturgical commemoration of the following calendar day at sunset. Violakis codifies the exact structural rules for integrating the variable propers: when the day is a Feast of the Lord, of the Mother of God, or a celebrated Saint, the festal Troparion and Kontakion are recited; on simple weekdays without a festal Kontakion, the ancient penitential troparia are recited: **"The thief, beholding the Author of life"** (*Βλέπων ὁ Λῃστὴς τὸν ἀρχηγὸν τῆς ζωῆς*), followed by *Glory...*, **"In the midst of two thieves"** (*Ἐν μέσῳ δύο λῃστῶν*), and *Now and forever...*, **"The Lamb and Shepherd"** (*Τὸν Ἀμνὸν καὶ Ποιμένα*). On Sunday evening, the Octoechos Ypakoe of the Tone replaces the Kontakion. Throughout Bright Week and on the eve of the Ascension, the entire office is replaced by the Paschal Ninth Hour (*«Ἀνάστασιν Χριστοῦ θεασάμενοι...»*), highlighting the uninterrupted festal joy of the Resurrection.

[^55]: The priest's liturgical preparation and vesting for the opening of Vespers (Violakis, p. 38, note 1). Before intoning the foundational exclamation **"Blessed is our God always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages"** (*Εὐλογητὸς ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν πάντοτε...*), the priest is canonically obligated to make three reverent prostrations (*metanias*) before the Holy Table, kiss the Holy Table and the hand-cross, and vest in the Epitrachilion (*ἐπιτραχήλιον*), reciting the prescribed vesting prayer. In accordance with the *Sluzhebnik* (*Ἱερατικόν*), the priest may not remove the Epitrachilion until the final dismissal of the service; Violakis strongly censures any laxity in this regard, declaring that a priest who fails to observe this order acts disorderly and unlawfully (*ἀτακτεῖ καὶ παρανομεῖ*).

[^56]: The canonical dispute regarding the opening exclamation of Bright Week Vespers (Violakis, p. 38, note 2). Violakis notes that ancient Sabbaite and Constantinopolitan Typika (including the Venetian printed Typikon of 1685) appoint the standard opening exclamation **"Blessed is our God always..."** (*Εὐλογητὸς ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν...*) even for the solemn Vespers of Bright Monday (the Agape Vespers), followed immediately by the Paschal troparion **"Christ is risen from the dead"** (*Χριστὸς ἀνέστη ἐκ νεκρῶν*) instead of the ferial invocations. Violakis classifies the modern practice of opening Paschal Vespers with the Trinitarian exclamation **"Glory to the Holy, Consubstantial, Life-Creating, and Undivided Trinity"** (*Δόξα τῇ Ἁγίᾳ καὶ Ὁμοουσίῳ...*) as an irregular post-Byzantine innovation (*παράτυπος νεωτερισμός*), though he acknowledges its virtually universal adoption in contemporary patriarchal, cathedral, and parish practice.

[^57]: The Introductory Psalm (*Προοιμιακὸς Ψαλμός*, Psalm 103 LXX: **"Bless the Lord, O my soul"** / *Εὐλόγει ἡ ψυχή μου τὸν Κύριον* / *Благослови, душе моя, Господа*). As the cosmic hymn of creation, Psalm 103 depicts the creation and providential sustenance of the universe by God the Word, setting the theological framework of the evening service as humanity's grateful contemplation of creation and longing for redemption. The psalm is omitted entirely only during Bright Week (from the Agape Vespers of Pascha Sunday through Bright Saturday) and at the Vespers of the Apodosis of Pascha, where the service opens directly with the Paschal Stichera and the Great Litany. From the Sunday of St. Thomas until the Apodosis of Pascha, the psalm is preceded not by the three-fold **"Come, let us worship"** (*Δεῦτε προσκυνήσωμεν*), but by **"Christ is risen"** chanted thrice (once by the clergy in the sanctuary and twice by the choirs).

[^58]: St. John Chrysostom's Homily on Psalm 41 [Psalm 41 LXX / Psalm 42 MT] (*Εἰς τὸν Ψαλμὸν ΜΑ΄*, PG 55:155–160; Violakis, pp. 40–41, note 3). Violakis appends this extensive patristic treatise to impress upon clergy and cantors the profound therapeutic, moral, and pneumatological efficacy of liturgical psalmody. Chrysostom argues that God, perceiving human weakness and reluctance toward spiritual instruction, mingled sweet melody with prophetic revelation, comparing this divine economy to wise physicians who smear the rim of a medicine cup with honey so that sick children will drink the curative potion. Sacred psalmody sanctifies the tongue, purges bodily passions, attracts the grace of the Holy Spirit, and repels demonic temptation, transforming both the temple and the domestic hearth into a true spiritual sanctuary (*Ἐκκλησία*).

[^59]: The First Kathisma of the Psalter (*Α΄ Κάθισμα τοῦ Ψαλτηρίου*, Psalms 1–8 LXX: **"Blessed is the man"** / *Μακάριος ἀνήρ* / *Блаженъ мужъ*) at Saturday Great Vespers. In the Sabbaite monastic tradition, the entire Psalter is chanted weekly, with the First Kathisma appointed for Saturday Vespers as the opening of the Sunday liturgical cycle. Violakis codifies the parish practice of the Great Church: the Kathisma is omitted whenever a Despotic Feast (Feast of the Lord) falls on Sunday—namely, the Nativity of Christ (December 25), Theophany (January 6), and the Transfiguration (August 6)—as well as on Pentecost Sunday, because the festive propers entirely replace the weekly psalmody. However, on Palm Sunday, St. Thomas Sunday, and the Exaltation of the Holy Cross falling on Sunday, the Kathisma is chanted, while the Resurrectional hymns of the Octoechos are omitted.

[^60]: The First Stasis of the Psalter (*Α΄ Στάσις τοῦ Ψαλτηρίου*, Psalms 1–3 LXX) on weekday feasts, accompanied by the Patristic Catena from Basil the Great, Theodoret of Cyrus, and Gregory of Nyssa (Violakis, pp. 42–43). On Feasts of the Mother of God and Polyeleos-rank Saints falling on Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, or Friday, the first stasis of the First Kathisma (Psalms 1–3 LXX: **"Blessed is the man"** / *Μακάριος ἀνήρ*) is chanted at Vespers. The patristic catena emphasizes: St. Basil (*Homily on Psalm 1*, PG 29:209–212) defining psalmody as the calm of souls, arbitrator of peace, and voice of the Church; Theodoret of Cyrus (*Interpretatio in Psalmos*, PG 80:860) praising divine grace that blended pleasure with spiritual profit; and Gregory of Nyssa (*In Inscriptiones Psalmorum*, PG 44:433) demonstrating how David blended melody with virtue to pour sweet honey upon sublime doctrines.

[^61]: The dual liturgical character of the Meeting of the Lord (*Ὑπαπαντὴ τοῦ Κυρίου*, February 2) and the Annunciation (*Εὐαγγελισμὸς τῆς Θεοτόκου*, March 25) (Violakis, pp. 44–45, note 4). Although historically grouped among the Feasts of the Mother of God, both feasts commemorate events central to the Incarnation of our Lord Jesus Christ, and therefore possess the liturgical rank, privileges, and structural characteristics of Feasts of the Lord (*Δεσποτικαὶ ἑορταί*). Consequently, when either feast falls on a Saturday or Monday, the First Stasis of the Psalter is omitted at Vespers, in accordance with the statutory rule governing Despotic Feasts.

[^62]: The Lucernarium Psalmody (*Κύριε ἐκέκραξα*, Psalms 140, 141, 129, and 116 LXX) and the evening censing ritual (Violakis, pp. 44–45, note 5). In daily Vespers, all verses of the Lucernarium psalms are chanted in the appointed Tone of the week [Tone 1–8]. On Great Feasts, parish practice omits the intermediate verses from **"Set, O Lord, a watch before my mouth"** (*Θοῦ, Κύριε, φυλακήν*), jumping directly to the verses matching the number of appointed Stichera (10, 8, or 6). When the Second Choir begins the second verse, **"Let my prayer be set forth before Thee as incense"** (*Κατευθυνθήτω ἡ προσευχή μου*), the priest blesses incense and censes the Holy Table on all four sides, the sanctuary, the iconostasis, and the entire congregation. If an Entrance (*Εἴσοδος*) with the censer is appointed, the priest vests in the Phelonion (*φελώνιον*) at the beginning of *Κύριε ἐκέκραξα*; if no Entrance is appointed, he censes wearing only the Epitrachilion.

[^63]: The canonical precedence of the Resurrectional Octoechos on Sundays. Throughout the entire liturgical year, Sunday services (Saturday Great Vespers and Sunday Matins) belong primarily to the celebration of the Lord's Resurrection. The Resurrectional hymns of the Octoechos (Stichera, Doxastika, Dismissal Troparia, Kathismata, Ypakoe, Anavathmoi, Canons, Kontakia, Ikoses, Exapostilaria, Eothina Gospels, and Beatitudes) hold absolute priority, preceding the propers of the Mother of God and the commemorations of Saints. Violakis codifies the exact hierarchical ranking governing how festal, post-festal, and sanctoral hymns are intercalated with the weekly Resurrectional propers.


## Cohort 7 Footnotes

[^64]: Distribution of the Lucernarium Stichera (*Κύριε ἐκέκραξα*) on Sundays and Feasts (Violakis, pp. 46–47, §7). The Great Church of Christ maintains a rigorous mathematical hierarchy for the distribution of the ten evening Stichera: on principal Feasts of the Mother of God and on their Apodoses, the festal theme takes pre-eminence after the initial Resurrection proclamation (4 Resurrection + 6 Feast); on ordinary Afterfeast Sundays and commemorations of celebrated Saints (Polyeleos or Doxology rank), the Resurrection holds majority weight (6 Resurrection + 4 Feast or Saint); when a celebrated Saint falls on an Afterfeast Sunday or an Apodosis, a three-way division is observed (4 Resurrection + 3 Feast + 3 Saint). For major patristic and apostolic celebrations (the Archangels, the Forerunner, Peter and Paul, John the Theologian, the Three Hierarchs, the Ecumenical Councils, Demetrius, and Nicholas), the Saint is honored with 6 Stichera against 4 Resurrection Stichera. On Great Feasts of the Lord (Nativity, Theophany, Palm Sunday, St. Thomas Sunday, Pentecost, Transfiguration, and Exaltation of the Cross), the Resurrection hymns are entirely suppressed in favor of the festal propers.

[^65]: The First Evening Doxastikon and Dogmatikon (*Τὸ α΄ Θεοτοκίον τοῦ Ἤχου / Δογματικόν*) (Violakis, pp. 46–49, §8). In the Constantinopolitan and Sabbaite synthesis, the evening *Doxastikon* (*Δοξαστικόν*) occupies the summit of the Lucernarium hymnody. When a Feast of the Mother of God falls on Sunday, its own festal idiomelon serves for both *Glory...* and *Now and forever...*. On Afterfeast Sundays, Apodoses, and commemorations of celebrated Saints, the festal or sanctoral idiomelon is chanted at *Glory...*, followed invariably at *Now and forever...* by the First Theotokion of the Tone (*Τὸ α΄ Θεοτοκίον τοῦ ἤχου*) from the Octoechos, known as the *Dogmatikon* (*Δογματικόν*). Composed by St. John of Damascus, these eight dogmatic hymns expound the mystery of the Incarnation and the two natures in Christ. Violakis details the precise rules for intercalating the Triodion and Pentecostarion Doxastika during Great Lent and Paschaltide, preserving the ancient hierarchy between movable and immovable cycles.

[^66]: The Saturday Vespers Prokimenon and the Great Prokimena (Violakis, pp. 48–49, §9). The universal Prokimenon of Saturday Great Vespers is Psalm 92:1 LXX: **"The Lord is King, He is robed in majesty"** (*Ὁ Κύριος ἐβασίλευσεν, εὐπρέπειαν ἐνεδύσατο*), chanted in Tone 6 with its four appointed verses. Even when major feasts of the Lord or of the Mother of God fall on Sunday, this psalmody is maintained, affirming the primordial Sunday celebration of the Resurrection. The sole exceptions are the afterfeast Vespers of Great Feasts of the Lord (such as Sunday evening of Pascha, Pentecost, Nativity, and Theophany), at which the Great Prokimena in Tone 7 are chanted: **"Who is a god as great as our God?"** (*Τίς θεὸς μέγας*, Psalm 76:14 LXX) and **"Our God is in heaven and on earth"** (*Ὁ δὲ θεὸς ἡμῶν*, Psalm 113:11 LXX). At the Vespers of Holy Saturday, connected with the ancient baptismal Liturgy of St. Basil the Great, the standard Prokimenon is replaced after the Epistle (Romans 6:3–11) by Psalm 81:8 LXX: **"Arise, O God, judge the earth"** (*Ἀνάστα ὁ Θεός*), during which the clergy exchange their dark vestments for white robes.

[^67]: Old Testament Prophecies and Liturgical Epistles at Vespers (Violakis, pp. 48–51, §10). At Saturday Great Vespers, three Old Testament readings (*Paroimiai* / *Παροιμίαι*) are appointed whenever a Feast of the Lord, a Feast of the Mother of God, or a celebrated Saint occurs on Sunday. These readings are introduced by the Deacon's solemn exclamation **"Wisdom! Let us be attentive!"** (*Σοφία, πρόσχωμεν!*). A distinctive archaic feature preserved in the Byzantine Typikon is the chanting of New Testament Apostolic Epistles (*Ἀπόστολος*) at Great Vespers on the feasts of prime Apostles—Peter and Paul (June 29), John the Theologian (May 8), James the Brother of the Lord (October 23), and Andrew the First-Called (November 30). These are delivered with the full solemn liturgical cantillation of the Divine Liturgy, preceded by the Deacon's double exclamation **"Let us be attentive! Wisdom! Let us be attentive!"** (*Πρόσχωμεν, σοφία, πρόσχωμεν!*), witnessing to the ancient cathedral vigil practice of Constantinople.

[^68]: The Rite of the Artoklasia at the Litiya (*Ἡ τάξις τῆς Ἀρτοκλασίας ἐν τῇ Λιτῇ*) (Violakis, pp. 50–51, §11). Derived from the ancient monastic all-night vigil (*Agrypnia*), the Artoklasia (lit. "breaking of bread") provides physical nourishment and spiritual benediction during extended liturgical services. Following the prayer of the bowing of heads, the clergy proceed in solemn procession to the nave before the Tetrapod, where five round wheat loaves, wheat grains, wine, and olive oil are prepared in a specialized four-branched cruet-stand (*artophorion* / *tetrapodion*). The Deacon intones the expansive Litiya intercessions for ecclesiastical, civil, and global preservation. The Priest incenses the offerings crosswise while chanting the troparion **"O Mother of God and Virgin, rejoice"** (*Θεοτόκε Παρθένε*), replaced during Bright Week by the Paschal Troparion **"Christ is risen from the dead"** (*Χριστὸς ἀνέστη*). The rite concludes with the solemn blessing prayer invoking the multiplication of the five loaves in the wilderness (Matthew 14:13–21) and the chanting of Psalm 33:11 LXX: **"The rich have become poor and gone hungry"** (*Πλούσιοι ἐπτώχευσαν*).

[^69]: The Invariant Priority of the Resurrection Aposticha at Saturday Vespers (Violakis, pp. 50–53, §12). The Resurrection Stichera at the Aposticha (*Ἀπόστιχα Ἀναστάσιμα*) of Saturday Vespers form an inviolable structural bedrock in the Byzantine Rite: they are never suppressed, even on Feasts of the Mother of God, Forefeast or Afterfeast Sundays, Apodoses, or celebrations of Polyeleos-rank Saints. During the post-Paschal period (the four Sundays following St. Thomas Sunday: the Myrrh-Bearers, the Paralytic, the Samaritan Woman, and the Blind Man), the First Choir intones the first Resurrection Apostichon of the Tone, followed immediately by the four triumphant Paschal Stichera **"A sacred Pascha"** (*Πάσχα ἱερὸν*) with their biblical verses from Psalm 67 LXX (**"Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered"**), synthesizing the weekly Sunday Octoechos with the unwaning joy of the Fifty Days.

[^70]: The Doxastikon and Theotokion of the Aposticha (Violakis, pp. 52–55, §13). At the Aposticha, the concluding hymnody balances festal commemoration with Marian theology. On Feasts of the Mother of God, Afterfeast Sundays, Feasts of the Lord, and specific commemorative Sundays (such as the Sunday of the Holy Cross or the Sunday of the Myrrh-Bearers), the festal Doxastikon is chanted alone without a concluding Theotokion. When a celebrated Saint is commemorated on Sunday, the Saint's Doxastikon is chanted at *Glory...*, followed at *Now and forever...* by the Aposticha Theotokion from the Octoechos in the same Tone as the Saint's hymn. During the post-Paschal season on weekdays, the Doxastikon of the Saint is paired at *Now and forever...* with the Doxastikon of the preceding Sunday from the Pentecostarion, strictly reserving the great Paschal doxastikon **"It is the day of Resurrection"** (*Ἀναστάσεως ἡμέρα*) for Saturday Vespers and Sunday Matins.

[^71]: The Canticle of Simeon and the Paschal Substitution (Violakis, pp. 54–55, §14). The Canticle of the Righteous Simeon (*Νῦν ἀπολύεις*, Luke 2:29–32) and the Trisagion Prayers (*Τρισάγιον*) form the standard concluding hinge of Byzantine Vespers, leading directly into the Dismissal Troparia. Throughout Bright Week and on the eve of the Ascension (the Vespers of the Apodosis of Pascha), the entire penitential and nocturnal character of this liturgical juncture is transfigured: following the Paschal Doxastikon **"It is the day of Resurrection"** (*Ἀναστάσεως ἡμέρα*), the Trisagion Prayers are omitted completely, and **"Christ is risen from the dead"** (*Χριστὸς ἀνέστη*) is chanted thrice in its standard festive melody, moving immediately into the sacerdotal dismissal.

[^72]: The Hierarchy and Intercalation of Dismissal Troparia at Saturday Vespers (Violakis, pp. 54–55, §15). The Dismissal Troparia (*Ἀπολυτίκια*) sung at the conclusion of Saturday Great Vespers and at Matins at **"God is the Lord"** govern the theological thematic hierarchy of the day. The Resurrection Troparion of the Octoechos always holds absolute precedence, chanted first before any festal or sanctoral troparia. On principal Feasts of the Mother of God and on their Apodoses, the Resurrection Troparion is chanted once and the festal troparion twice (totaling three troparia); on ordinary Afterfeast Sundays, each is chanted once at Vespers, whereas at Matins the Resurrection Troparion is chanted twice and the festal troparion once. When complex coincidences arise—such as the Annunciation falling on the Third Sunday of Lent, Lazarus Saturday, or Palm Sunday—Violakis details the precise triple intercalation order, ensuring that neither the Paschal journey, the cross, nor the incarnation is eclipsed.


## Cohort 8 Footnotes

[^73]: The Sacerdotal Dismissal (*Τελεία / Μεγάλη Ἀπόλυσις*) and Weekday Adaptation (Violakis, pp. 56–57, §16, Notes 6 & 7). In §16, Violakis codifies the full ceremonial structure of the Major Dismissal at the conclusion of Saturday Great Vespers. The rite begins with the diaconal exclamation **"Wisdom!"** (*Σοφία*), the sacerdotal doxology **"Blessed is He Who is, Christ our God"** (*Ὁ ὢν εὐλογητὸς Χριστὸς ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν*), and the ancient prayer for the preservation of the Orthodox faith and the Church intoned by the Superior (**"Establish, O Lord God, the holy Orthodox faith..."** [*Στερεώσαι Κύριος ὁ Θεὸς τὴν ἁγίαν πίστιν...*]). When an Artoklasia has taken place, the dialogue is adapted: the Deacon intones **"Let us pray to the Lord"** (*Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν*), and the Bishop or Priest imparts the blessing **"The blessing of the Lord and His mercy come upon you..."** (*Εὐλογία Κυρίου καὶ ἔλεος αὐτοῦ...*). In Note 6, Violakis records that this identical Dismissal formula is employed on the remaining days of the week, with the sole omission of the initial Resurrection clause **"May He Who rose from the dead"** (*Ὁ ἀναστὰς ἐκ νεκρῶν*). In Note 7, he observes that ancient Typika designate this full rite as the "Complete" or "Perfect Dismissal" (*τελεία Ἀπόλυσις*), distinguishing it from abbreviated forms.

[^74]: Festal Characteristic Invocations (*Χαρακτηριστικὰ τῆς Ἀπολύσεως*) and the Minor Dismissal (*Μικρὰ Ἀπόλυσις*) (Violakis, pp. 56–59, §16). The Major Dismissal incorporates distinct historical incipits reflecting the theological mystery commemorated on Great Feasts of the Lord (e.g. Nativity: **"He Who was born in a cave and lay in a manger"**; Theophany: **"He Who deigned to be baptized in the Jordan by John for our salvation"**). On the Feast of the Universal Exaltation of the Holy Cross (September 14), the Resurrection phrase **"He Who rose from the dead"** is preserved regardless of the day of the week, anchored in the Matins hymn **"Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ"** (*Ἀνάστασιν Χριστοῦ θεασάμενοι*). Violakis notes that among Feasts of the Mother of God, only the Annunciation (March 25) and the Meeting of the Lord (February 2) possess proper characteristic dismissal invocations, which are intercalated into the Sunday Resurrection dismissal when falling on Sunday. Paragraph 16 also defines the Minor Dismissal (*μικρὰ Ἀπόλυσις*), utilized at the Little Hours, Small Vespers, Compline, and the Midnight Office. This form condenses the intercessory catalog, invoking only the Theotokos, the Holy Apostles, and the Saint of the day, followed either immediately by **"Through the prayers..."** (*Δι᾽ εὐχῶν*) or by the litany **"Let us pray for the peace of the world..."** (*Εὐξώμεθα ὑπὲρ εἰρήνης...*).

[^75]: Patristic Theology of Nocturnal Vigils: St. Basil the Great on Antiphonal Psalmody (Violakis, pp. 58–61, §17, Note 8). At the inception of the rubrics for Matins (*Ὄρθρος*), Violakis incorporates a foundational patristic testimony from St. Basil the Great's Epistle 207 (al. 63/67, *Ad clericos Neocaesarienses*, Migne PG 32:759–764). Writing to defend the ascetic vigil practices of his Cappadocian community against charges of liturgical novelty, St. Basil witnesses to the universal fourth-century monastic practice of nocturnal prayer and antiphonal psalmody: the faithful assemble before dawn in the house of prayer, make confession to God in labor, sorrow, and tears, and then arise to sing psalms antiphonally (*διχῇ διανεμηθέντες ἀντιψάλλουσιν ἀλλήλοις*), led by a precentor (*ἑνὶ κατάρχειν τοῦ μέλους*). At daybreak, all offer up in unison the 50th Psalm of confession (Psalm 50 LXX). St. Basil appeals to the common consensus of Egypt, the Thebaid, Libya, Palestine, Arabia, Phoenicia, Syria, and Mesopotamia, demonstrating that nocturnal vigils and psalmody constitute the universal heritage of the Catholic Church.

[^76]: The Sunday Midnight Office (*Μεσονυκτικόν*) and the Monastic Absolution Rite (*Εὐξώμεθα ὑπὲρ εἰρήνης*) (Violakis, pp. 60–61, §17, Note 9). On Sundays, the penitential 17th Kathisma (Psalm 118, *Amomos*) of the daily Midnight Office is replaced by an order celebrating the Holy Trinity: following the introductory prayers and Psalm 50 LXX, the Triadic Canon (*Τριαδικὸς κανών*) from the Octoechos is chanted with the refrain **"Most Holy Trinity, our God, have mercy on us and save us,"** followed by the four Triadic Troparia **"It is truly meet"** (*Ἄξιόν ἐστιν*), the Trisagion Prayers, and the Hypakoe (*Ὑπακοή*) of the Tone. Following the Minor Dismissal, the Deacon or Priest intones the litany of mutual intercession **"Let us pray for the peace of the world..."** (*Εὐξώμεθα ὑπὲρ εἰρήνης τοῦ κόσμου...*). In Note 9, Violakis explains that this concluding petition is an ancient survival of monastic coenobitic discipline (*τῆς μοναχικῆς τάξεως λείψανον*), wherein the monastics prostrated and sought mutual forgiveness (*metanoia*) from the Superior (*proestos*) before departing, as detailed further in §50 of the Protheoria.

[^77]: Festal and Seasonal Transformations of the Midnight Office (Violakis, pp. 60–63, §17). Violakis details the rubrical adjustments required when major commemorations fall on a Sunday: if a Feast of the Mother of God or a celebrated Saint occurs, the Litiya hymns of the feast are chanted after the Triadic Canon (or after Psalm 50 on Great Feasts of the Lord), and the Hypakoe is replaced by the festal Dismissal Troparion. During Great Lent (Sundays of the Triodion), the Hypakoe is replaced by the three Penitential Troparia (**"Have mercy on us, O Lord, have mercy on us"** [*Ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς, Κύριε, ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς*]), except on the Third Sunday of Lent (Veneration of the Cross) or when the Meeting of the Lord or the Annunciation coincides. Throughout Bright Week and on the Apodosis of Pascha, the entire Midnight Office is replaced by the Paschal Nocturns (**"Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ... Anticipating the dawn..."** [*Ἀνάστασιν Χριστοῦ... Προλαβοῦσαι τὸν ὄρθρον*]). An editorial correction in the Greek text notes that Violakis's cross-reference to §3, Note 4 (Easter Vespers) is an erratum for §2, Note 8 (Easter Divine Liturgy).

[^78]: The Royal Office (*Βασιλικὰ Τροπάρια*) and the Six Psalms (*Ἑξάψαλμος*) at the Threshold of Matins (Violakis, pp. 62–63, §18). Matins proper begins with the Priest entering the Sanctuary and making the solemn doxology before the Holy Table: **"Blessed is our God, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages."** The Reader recites the Trisagion Prayers and the Royal Troparia (**"O Lord, save Thy people"**, **"Thou Who wast lifted up upon the Cross"**, and the Theotokion **"O fearsome Protection"**), commemorating Christ as King and invoking divine protection over the Christian realm. Following a short litany, the Priest proclaims the solemn doxology **"Glory to the Holy, Consubstantial, Life-Creating, and Undivided Trinity..."**, after which the Superior reads the Six Psalms (*Ἑξάψαλμος*: Psalms 3, 37, 62, 87, 102, and 142 LXX). In Byzantine liturgical theology, the Hexapsalmos represents the stillness of the nocturnal expectation of the Final Judgment; it is delivered with profound compunction in total silence, and is never omitted except during Bright Week and on the Apodosis of Pascha.

[^79]: The Chanting of "God is the Lord" and the Intercalation of Dismissal Troparia (Violakis, pp. 62–65, §19). The proclamation **"God is the Lord, and hath appeared unto us; blessed is He that cometh in the Name of the Lord"** (Psalm 117:27, 26 LXX) marks the liturgical announcement of the Incarnation at the dawn of Matins. It is chanted four times in the Tone of the week, punctuated by three verses from Psalm 117 LXX: (1) **"Give thanks unto the Lord, and call upon His holy Name"** (v. 1); (2) **"All nations compassed me about..."** (v. 10); and (3) **"This is the Lord's doing, and it is marvelous in our eyes"** (v. 23). The Dismissal Troparia (*Ἀπολυτίκια*) are intercalated immediately thereafter: on ordinary Sundays, the Resurrection Troparion is chanted twice, followed at **"Now and forever..."** by its Theotokion; on Feasts of the Mother of God, the Resurrection Troparion is chanted once and the festal troparion twice; on Afterfeast Sundays, the Resurrection Troparion is doubled and the festal troparion sung once; and when a celebrated Saint occurs, all three troparia (Resurrection, Saint, and Feast) are chanted once each.

[^80]: The Lenten Alleluia and the Nocturnal Canticle of Isaiah (Violakis, pp. 64–65, §19). On fasting weekdays of Great Lent (Monday through Friday), the festal proclamation **"God is the Lord"** is suppressed and replaced by **"Alleluia"** (*Ἀλληλούϊα*) chanted four times (thrice after each verse) in Tone 8 [Tone 4 Plagal]. The four verses are taken from the Fifth Biblical Ode, the Canticle of the Prophet Isaiah (Isaiah 26:9–15 LXX): (1) **"From the night my spirit waketh early unto Thee, O God, for Thy commandments are a light upon the earth"** (v. 9); (2) **"Learn righteousness, ye that dwell upon the earth"** (v. 9); (3) **"Jealousy shall seize upon an untaught people, and now fire shall devour the adversaries"** (v. 11); and (4) **"Add evils unto them, O Lord, add evils unto them, unto the glorious of the earth"** (v. 15). This ancient penitential substitution emphasizes vigilance, judgment, and compunction, replacing the festive joy of the dawn with ascetic longing for the light of Christ.

[^81]: The Memorial Alleluia of Saturday and the Ancestral Soul Saturdays (*Σάββατα τῶν Ψυχῶν*) (Violakis, pp. 64–65, §19). On ordinary Saturdays throughout the year that lack the commemoration of an illustrious Saint, the ancestral Sabbath character of resting in Christ is manifested by replacing **"God is the Lord"** with the Memorial **"Alleluia"** in Tone 2, accompanied by the appointed verses for the departed: **"Blessed are they whom Thou hast chosen and taken to Thyself, O Lord"** (Psalm 64:5 LXX) and **"And their memorial is from generation to generation"** (Psalm 101:13 LXX). These verses are followed by the general troparia for martyrs and the departed: **"Apostles, Martyrs, and Prophets"** and **"Remember, O Lord, as Thou art good, Thy servants."** On the two universal Saturdays of Souls (*Σάββατα τῶν Ψυχῶν*)—Meatfare Saturday and the Saturday before Pentecost—the services are dedicated entirely to the memory of all departed Orthodox Christians from the ages, featuring the classic funeral Apolytikion in Tone 8: **"O Thou Who with profound wisdom and love for mankind orderest all things..."** (*Ὁ βάθει σοφίας*).


## Cohort 9 Footnotes

[^82]: The Liturgical Stichologia of the Psalter and the Polyeleos vs. Amomos Distinction (Violakis, pp. 66–67, §20). In §20, Violakis details the ancient monastic and cathedral discipline regarding the Psalter at Matins. Traditionally, the appointed Kathismata of the Psalter are chanted in verses (*stichologountai*) prior to the Sessional Hymns (Kathismata) of each of the two (or three) stichologiae. By pastoral economy (*oikonomia chronou*), contemporary parish practice often transfers the reading of the prescribed Kathismata of the Psalter, the Polyeleos, or the Amomos (Psalm 118 LXX) before the beginning of Matins. Violakis delineates the scriptural structure of the Polyeleos (*Πολυέλεος*): for Feasts of the Lord, Psalms 134 and 135 LXX (**"Praise the Name of the Lord"** and **"Give thanks unto the Lord, for He is good"**); for Feasts of the Mother of God, Psalm 44 LXX (**"My heart hath poured forth a good word"**); and for celebrated Saints, proper festal selections from the *Eklogarion* (or Psalms 134–135 LXX as default). On the Pre-Lenten Meatfare and Cheesefare Sundays, Psalm 136 LXX (**"By the rivers of Babylon"** [*Ἐπὶ τῶν ποταμῶν Βαβυλῶνος*]) is added to evoke the sorrow of exile from Paradise. The annual distribution mandates two Kathismata daily and three during Great Lent, culminating in completing the Psalter twice during the First Week of Lent, and once between Palm Sunday Vespers and Holy Wednesday. In accordance with universal Byzantine typika, psalmody ceases entirely from Holy Thursday until Saturday of Bright Week.

[^83]: The Architecture of Sessional Hymns (Kathismata) at Matins (Violakis, pp. 66–69, §21). Paragraph 21 establishes the rules governing the hymnographic Kathismata (*Καθίσματα*, Sessional Hymns) that follow the Psalter stichologiae at Matins. On Sundays coinciding with Marian Feasts, the first two stichologiae combine two Resurrection Kathismata and one Festal Kathisma, displacing the ordinary Octoechos Theotokia; the third stichologia is reserved exclusively for the Festal Kathisma following the Polyeleos. On Afterfeast Sundays and Apodoses, the third stichologia Kathisma is suppressed, and festal troparia replace the Octoechos Theotokia at the first two stichologiae. On commemorations of celebrated Saints with Polyeleos (the Holy Apostles, the Forerunner, the Three Hierarchs, Great Martyrs), the Saint's Kathisma is intercalated into each of the first two stichologiae after the Resurrection Kathisma and before the Theotokion, and the full third stichologia is chanted following the Polyeleos.

[^84]: The Sunday Resurrectional Complex: Evlogitaria, Hypakoe, Anavathmoi, and Matins Gospel (Violakis, pp. 68–69, §22). In §22, Violakis codifies the core resurrectional sequence of Sunday Matins: following the Kathismata, the Resurrectional Evlogitaria (**"Blessed art Thou, O Lord, teach me Thy statutes"** [*Εὐλογητὸς εἶ, Κύριε*], Psalm 118:12 LXX) are chanted, followed by the Little Litany, the sacerdotal exclamation **"For blessed is Thy Name..."**, the Hypakoe (*Ὑπακοή*) of the Tone, the Gradual Antiphons (*Ἀναβαθμοί*), the Sunday Prokimenon, and the Canons. If a Feast of the Mother of God coincides with Sunday, the Evlogitaria are omitted, and the order transitions directly to the Little Litany, Hypakoe, Gradual Antiphons, and the Festal Prokimenon. In this festal order, the Matins Gospel of the Feast is proclaimed, followed by **"Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ"** (*Ἀνάστασιν Χριστοῦ θεασάμενοι*), Psalm 50 LXX read *chuma* (smoothly/non-melodically), **"Through the intercessions of the Theotokos"**, and the festal Idiomelon. On Afterfeasts and Apodoses of Dominical or Marian Feasts falling on Sunday, however, the resurrectional Eothinon (Morning) Gospel of the weekly cycle takes precedence and is strictly preserved.

[^85]: Resurrectional Primacy of the Octoechos Canons and Seasonal Exceptions (Violakis, pp. 68–71, §23). Paragraph 23 sets forth the inviolable canonical principle that the Sunday Resurrection Canons of the Octoechos are never wholly omitted, even when coinciding with Marian Feasts or Apodoses. Violakis catalogs the specific historical exceptions: the Third Sunday of Lent (Veneration of the Holy Cross) and the Sunday before Christmas (Holy Fathers/Genealogy), where the Canons of the day replace the Octoechos Canons, but their initial troparia following the Heirmos are resurrectional and intoned with the verse **"Glory to Thy holy Resurrection, O Lord."** Similarly, on the four Sundays following Thomas Sunday (Myrrhbearers, Paralytic, Samaritan Woman, Blind Man), the Octoechos Canons are superseded by the Paschal Canon of St. John of Damascus with its two Theotokia. When a Marian Feast or Apodosis falls on Sunday, the Octoechos Canon is chanted first to four verses, followed by the festal Canon (or Canons to twelve verses). For weekday commemorations of Saints, the Small Paraklesis Canon of the Theotokos is chanted alongside the Saint's Canon, except for twelve major feast days of Apostles, Evangelists, and Hierarchs which possess proper Marian Canons or Pentecostarion/festal antecedents.

[^86]: From Biblical Canticles to Troparion Refrains: The Evolution of Canon Stichologia (Violakis, pp. 70–73, §24). Violakis documents the liturgical transition from the ancient practice of chanting the Nine Biblical Odes (*αἱ ἐννέα Ὠδαί*)—the canticles of Moses, Hannah, Habakkuk, Isaiah, Jonah, the Three Youths, the Magnificat, and the Benedictus—to the abbreviated modern usage where the biblical verses are replaced by repetitive stichic refrains prefixed to each troparion. The canonical refrains are: **"Glory to Thy holy Resurrection, O Lord"** for Resurrection Canons; **"Most Holy Theotokos, save us"** for Marian Canons; **"Saints of God, intercede for us"** for Saints' Canons; **"Have mercy on me, O God, have mercy on me"** for the Great Penitential Canon of St. Andrew of Crete; and selected verses from Psalms 64, 24, and 101 LXX for funeral troparia. On Great Feasts of the Lord, because the biblical canticles are omitted entirely, no verses are sung before the troparia, save for **"Glory..."** and **"Now and forever..."** before the final two troparia. Throughout Afterfeasts of Dominical Feasts, the refrain **"Glory to Thee, our God, glory to Thee"** is prefixed to the festal troparia.

[^87]: Liturgical Articulation at the Third Ode: Kathisma and Kontakion Precedence (Violakis, pp. 72–73, §25). The conclusion of the Third Ode of the Canon marks an ancient liturgical junction at Matins. Following the Little Litany and the priestly exclamation, the Kathisma (Sessional Hymn) and Theotokion of the Saint are chanted. If a Feast of the Mother of God falls on Sunday, the Kontakion and Ikos of the Octoechos are transferred to the Third Ode and read prior to the Festal Kathisma, ensuring that the resurrectional commemoration is preserved while granting the post-ode sessional hymnody to the Feast. The same transfer rule governs all Sundays of the Triodion and commemorations of major celebrated Saints. On Forefeast and Afterfeast Sundays, the Kathisma of the Saint is chanted first, followed by that of the Forefeast or Afterfeast.

[^88]: The Climax of the Canon: The Sixth Ode Kontakion and Ikos Transfer Rules (Violakis, pp. 72–75, §26). Paragraph 26 defines the placement of the Kontakion and Ikos at the conclusion of the Sixth Ode of Matins, preceded by the Little Litany and the exclamation **"For Thou art the King of peace..."** (*Ὅτι Σὺ εἶ ὁ Βασιλεὺς τῆς εἰρήνης*). On ordinary Sundays, the Kontakion and Ikos of the Octoechos are read. Violakis catalogs the extensive system of festal exceptions where the Octoechos Kontakion is displaced by that of the feast or seasonal commemoration: Marian Feasts, Triodion Sundays (except the 2nd, 4th, and 5th Sundays of Lent, which retain the Octoechos), the Pentecostarion Sundays, the Ancestral and Pre-Nativity Sundays (Forefathers, Sunday before/after Nativity), the Synods of the Holy Fathers (Nicaea I, Chalcedon, Nicaea II), and major saints whose feasts transfer the Resurrection Kontakion to the Third Ode. Furthermore, during the Pentecostarion, the Kontakia of Saints George, John the Theologian, and Constantine and Helen replace the Resurrection Kontakion at the Sixth Ode whenever their feasts coincide with a Sunday (excluding Thomas Sunday, Pentecost, and All Saints).

[^89]: The Katavasia Cycle: "I Shall Open My Mouth" and the Seasonal Typikon Stations (Violakis, pp. 74–75, §27). In §27, Violakis outlines the annual distribution of the Katavasiae (*Καταβασίαι*)—the concluding heirmoi sung by both choirs descending (*katabainein*) to the center of the nave. The normative Marian Katavasiae in Tone 4, **"I shall open my mouth, and it shall be filled with the Spirit"** (*Ἀνοίξω τὸ στόμα μου*), serve as the foundational default for all non-festal intervals of the liturgical year: (1) from the Apodosis of the Universal Exaltation of the Holy Cross (September 21) until the Forefeast of the Entry of the Theotokos (November 21); (2) from the Apodosis of the Meeting of the Lord (February 9) until the beginning of the Triodion, as well as on the 2nd, 4th, and 5th Sundays of Lent; and (3) from the Sunday of All Saints until July 27. On Sundays within Forefeasts and Afterfeasts, proper festal Katavasiae supersede the general cycle. On the first four Sundays of the Triodion and the First Sunday of Lent, the Triodion Katavasiae are chanted. On weekdays of the Triodion when a Saint occurs, the Katavasiae **"I shall open my mouth"** are chanted, and the Marian Kontakion **"O Protection of Christians unashamed"** (*Προστασία τῶν Χριστιανῶν*) is appointed for the Divine Liturgy.


## Cohort 10 Footnotes

[^90]: The Proclamation of the Resurrectional Morning Gospel at Sunday Matins and the Sacerdotal Intercession (*Σῶσον ὁ Θεός*) (Violakis, pp. 76–77, §28, Note 10). In §28, Violakis details the solemn liturgical complex following the Katavasia of the Eighth Ode on ordinary Sundays and Sundays of the Triodion. The Deacon (or Priest) opens the rite with the invitation **"Let us pray to the Lord"**, followed by the sacerdotal exclamation **"For Thou art holy, our God, and Thou dwellest among the saints..."** (*Ὅτι ἅγιος εἶ, ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν*), and the antiphonal proclamation of the Morning Prokimenon and **"Let every breath praise the Lord"** (*Πᾶσα πνοὴ αἰνεσάτω τὸν Κύριον*). The Priest proclaims the appointed Resurrectional Eothinon Gospel (from the fixed eleven-pericope cycle of the Resurrection apparitions), followed immediately by the ancient baptismal hymn **"Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ"** (*Ἀνάστασιν Χριστοῦ θεασάμενοι*), Psalm 50 LXX (**"Have mercy on me, O God"**), and the Pentecostal-resurrectional troparia (**"Through the intercessions of the Apostles... Through the intercessions of the Theotokos... Jesus, having risen from the grave..."**). On Sundays of the Triodion, these troparia are replaced by the compunctious penitential hymns of St. Andrew of Crete and St. Theodore the Studite: **"The doors of repentance do Thou open unto me, O Giver of Life"** (*Τῆς μετανοίας ἄνοιξόν μοι πύλας, Ζωοδότα*), **"Guide me in the paths of salvation, O Theotokos"**, and **"When I ponder the multitude of my evil deeds..."**. In Note 10, Violakis addresses a widespread liturgical abuse and pastoral anomaly: the sacerdotal intercession **"O God, save Thy people, and bless Thine inheritance..."** (*Σῶσον, ὁ Θεός, τὸν λαόν σου*) and its subsequent exclamation **"Through the mercies and compassions..."** belong strictly and canonically to the presiding Priest according to ancient Byzantine rubrics. However, because the Priest is frequently occupied in the Prothesis chapel performing the rite of Preparation (*Proskomide* / *Προσκομιδή*), this prayer was often delegated by concession to the Deacon. Violakis sternly reasserts the canonical principle: the rubrical order must be preserved (*ἡ τάξις ὅμως τηρητέα*), and the Priest is obliged to interrupt his preparation of the Holy Gifts at that precise liturgical junction, exit the Sanctuary or stand before the Holy Table, and personally intone the solemn intercession.

[^91]: The Stichologia of the Ninth Ode: The Biblical Canticle of the Theotokos vs. Festal Megalynaria (Violakis, pp. 76–79, §29). Paragraph 29 delineates the structural execution of the Ninth Ode at Matins. On normative days throughout the year—including ordinary Sundays, commemorations of celebrated Saints, Afterfeasts, and weekdays from Thomas Sunday to Pentecost—the Ninth Ode begins with the biblical Canticle of the Mother of God, the Magnificat (Luke 1:46–55), interspersed with the venerable Megalynarion composed by St. Cosmas the Melodist: **"More honorable than the Cherubim, and beyond compare more glorious than the Seraphim..."** (*Τὴν Τιμιωτέραν τῶν Χερουβίμ*), intoned according to the Tone of the day's Katavasiae. Violakis outlines the systematic exceptions where the Magnificat is suppressed in favor of festal Megalynaria (*Μεγαλυνάρια*) intercalated directly between the troparia of the Ninth Ode of the festal Canon: (1) Great Feasts of the Lord and of the Theotokos; (2) Holy Week; and (3) all Sundays from Pascha until Ascension. On the Apodoses (Leave-takings) of Dominical Feasts, the Ninth Ode is sung without **"More honorable"**, except for the Exaltation of the Holy Cross (September 14) and the Transfiguration (August 6) according to the Typikon of Constantine, which lack proper festal Megalynaria. On Marian Apodoses, if coinciding with Sunday, the Magnificat precedes the festal Ninth Ode and its Megalynaria; on weekdays, the festal Ninth Ode stands alone. Crucial idiosyncratic distinctions govern two major feasts: on the Apodosis of the Meeting of the Lord (February 9), because all fourteen festal Megalynaria are chanted, the Magnificat is completely displaced; on the Apodosis of the Annunciation (March 26), only the Magnificat is sung with the Heirmos and troparia, without festal Megalynaria. Finally, on the Feast of the Three Hierarchs (January 30), all three Ninth Odes are chanted in their entirety without the Magnificat, conforming to the Studite poly-heortological cathedral tradition (clarified in Violakis p. 167, §4, Note 4).

[^92]: The Sunday Trinitarian Doxology (*Ἅγιος Κύριος ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν*) and the Hierarchy of Exapostilaria (Violakis, pp. 78–81, §30, Notes * and 11). Paragraph 30 codifies the rubrics governing the Exapostilaria (*Ἐξαποστειλάρια*, hymns of "sending forth" at the dawn) and their introductory Trinitarian acclamation at Matins. Violakis establishes that the solemn singing of **"Holy is the Lord our God"** (*Ἅγιος Κύριος ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν*, Psalm 98:5, 9 LXX) is a unique, inviolable hallmark of Sunday Resurrectional Matins, never sung on weekdays even when commemorating celebrated Saints. On Sundays coinciding with Marian Feasts or Apodoses, the Sunday Resurrection Exapostilarion is chanted first, followed by the festal Exapostilarion twice. On Afterfeasts with celebrated Saints, the threefold progression is observed: (1) Resurrection, (2) Saint, and (3) Afterfeast. In Note 11, Violakis explains the performance practice of **"Holy is the Lord our God"**: it is chanted twice in the Tone of the Sunday Resurrection Exapostilarion, followed by the verse **"Exalt the Lord our God, and worship at His footstool, for He is holy"** (Psalm 98:5 LXX), whereafter the choir intones the Exapostilarion itself. On Palm Sunday and Holy Saturday, however, because neither service possesses a proper hymnographic Exapostilarion, **"Holy is the Lord our God"** is chanted three times without the psalm verse, functioning as the Exapostilarion itself. On Pascha and throughout Bright Week, the Paschal Exapostilarion **"Having fallen asleep in the flesh as a mortal..."** (*Σαρκὶ ὑπνώσας*) is chanted immediately without **"Holy is the Lord"**. During the Paschal season, Thomas Sunday features proper Exapostilaria: **"With thy hand, Thomas, probe My wounds..."** (*Ἐμῶν μελῶν χειρί σου*) and **"Today spring is fragrant..."** (*Σήμερον ἔαρ μυρίζει*), intercalating the Saint's Exapostilarion between them when one occurs.

[^93]: The Stichologia of the Praises (*Αἶνοι*): Combining Resurrectional, Festal, and Paschal Hymnody (Violakis, pp. 80–83, §31). In §31, Violakis details the numerical distribution of the Stichira at the Praises (*Ainoi*, Psalms 148, 149, 150 LXX) on Sundays coinciding with diverse liturgical cycles. The standard total allotment is eight stichira (intoned with the final four verses of Psalm 148/149 and four verses of Psalm 150 LXX). On Sundays of Marian Feasts or Apodoses, the choir divides the stichira equally: four Resurrectional Stichira of the Octoechos and four festal Stichira. When a celebrated Saint coincides with an Afterfeast or Apodosis Sunday, the tripartite distribution applies: three Resurrectional, two Afterfeast, and three of the Saint. If the Meeting of the Lord falls on one of the first four Sundays of the Triodion, Violakis distinguishes between the preparatory Sundays (Publican & Pharisee, Prodigal Son: 4 Resurrection + 4 Feast) and the strict pre-lenten Sundays (Meatfare, Cheesefare: 3 Resurrection + 2 Triodion + 3 Feast), preserving the thematic transition to Great Lent. For the Feast of the Annunciation falling on the Third Sunday of Lent (Veneration of the Cross), the Cross takes precedence: 2 Resurrection + 3 Cross + 3 Annunciation; if falling on the Fourth or Fifth Sunday of Lent, 4 Resurrection + 4 Annunciation. On the four Sundays following Thomas Sunday (Myrrhbearers, Paralytic, Samaritan Woman, Blind Man), commemorations of major saints (St. George, St. John the Theologian, Saints Constantine and Helen) introduce the Paschal Stichira: 3 Resurrection + 3 Saint, followed by the joyous Paschal Stichira **"A sacred Pascha hath been revealed to us today"** (*Πάσχα ἱερὸν ἡμῖν σήμερον ἀναδέδεικται*) with their proper Paschal verses (**"Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered..."**).

[^94]: The Eothinon Doxastikon of the Praises and Festal Displacements in Byzantine Cathedral Usage (Violakis, pp. 82–85, §32). Paragraph 32 codifies the theological and hymnographic role of the Eothinon Doxastikon (*Ἑωθινὸν Δοξαστικόν*)—the solemn hymn chanted at **"Glory to the Father..."** before the Great Doxology at Sunday Matins. Composed by the Byzantine Emperor Leo VI the Wise (886–912), the eleven Eothina Doxastika provide a profound liturgical commentary and theological synopsis of the eleven Sunday Morning Gospels. Under normative conditions, the Eothinon Doxastikon is chanted whenever the corresponding Morning Gospel has been proclaimed. Violakis catalogs the systematic exceptions where the Eothinon is displaced by a festal or hagiographical Doxastikon: (1) Feasts and Apodoses of the Theotokos and of the Lord; (2) all Sundays of the Triodion and Pentecostarion; (3) Pre-Nativity and Post-Nativity Sundays; (4) the Sundays of the Holy Fathers of the Ecumenical Councils (Nicaea I, Chalcedon, Nicaea II); (5) major feast days of the Forerunner, Apostles, Evangelists, and Hierarchs; and (6) patronal and titular feasts of the local temple. Significantly, Violakis records the authoritative ruling of Patriarch Constantius I of Constantinople (1830–1834): the Doxastika of St. Gregory the Theologian (January 25), St. John Chrysostom (November 13 / January 27), St. Andrew the First-Called (November 30), and the Great Martyr Euphemia (September 16 / July 11) must also supersede the Sunday Eothinon when falling on a Sunday, specifically honoring the Apostolic founder, the great Patriarchal Doctor-Fathers, and the Patroness and miracle-worker of the Ecumenical Patriarchate in Constantinople whose relics reside in the Patriarchal Church of St. George.

[^95]: The Marian Doxasticon Theotokion: "Most Blessed Art Thou" vs. the Paschal "Day of Resurrection" (Violakis, pp. 84–85, §33). Paragraph 33 governs the concluding Theotokion chanted at **"Now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** following the Doxastikon of the Praises and immediately preceding the Great Doxology. The universal Byzantine norm for Sunday Matins is the venerable Dogmatic Theotokion in Tone 2: **"Most blessed art thou, O Virgin Theotokos; for through Him Who was incarnate of thee, Hades is taken captive..."** (*Ὑπερευλογημένη ὑπάρχεις, Θεοτόκε Παρθένε*). Violakis notes that this hymn is so foundational to the Sunday resurrectional mystery that it is preserved even when a Feast of the Mother of God, or its Apodosis, or the Apodosis of a Feast of the Lord occurs on Sunday. The sole universal exception occurs during the post-Paschal season: on the four Sundays following Thomas Sunday (Myrrhbearers, Paralytic, Samaritan Woman, and Blind Man), **"Most blessed art thou"** is replaced by the climactic Paschal doxasticon composed by St. John of Damascus: **"The Day of Resurrection! Let us beam with festive joy, and let us embrace one another..."** (*Ἀναστάσεως ἡμέρα, καὶ λαμπρυνθῶμεν τῇ πανηγύρει*). When major Saints occur on these post-Paschal Sundays, the Sunday Theotokion is sung at **"Now and forever..."**, except on Myrrhbearers Sunday, where **"The Day of Resurrection"** is retained because the day's Doxastikon is itself an Eothinon hymn. On weekdays from Thomas Sunday until the Apodosis of Pascha, commemorations of these Saints appoint the Doxastikon of the preceding Sunday at **"Now and forever..."** rather than the Paschal hymn, preserving the strict hierarchical distinction between Lord's Days and ordinary ferias.


## Cohort 11 Footnotes

[^96]: The Marian Theotokia of the Praises across the Triodion, Holy Week, and Paschalion (Violakis, pp. 86–87, §33 cont.).
